To whom it may concem:

Lean confirm that Dr David Clarke is a post-doctoral researcher based at the National Centre for English
Cultural Tradition, University of Sheffield. During the past three years Dr Clarke has been collecting
material relating to the social history of the ‘UFO phenomenon’ in Britain from 1947 to present.

His research has concentrated upon documenting official investigations, drawing upon a wide range of
sources including material available at the Public Record Office and other public and private archives.

Dr Clarke is now in the process of preparing an application to the Economic and Social Research Council
(E.S.R.C.) for funding to undertake a two-year project to further develop his research.

The E.S.R.C. operates a joint scheme with MOD “designed to stimulate collaboration between research
establishments and academic institutes”, and notes that where research is concemed with “defence issues
and other areas which may be of interest to the MOD, the MOD may be willing to co-fund the project.”

The proposed research will document the historical context that has formed the basis for the development
of public policy towards the UFO problem from 1950 to present. It will scrutinize the role of the media and
other opinion-formers in shaping popular opinion towards “unidentified flying objects’, and discuss how
this in turn has influenced public policy adopted by MOD.

Iam confident that Dr Clarke’s proposal will be of interest to the Ministry of Defence as a historical
reference work, given the Ministry’s responsibility for answering public and media inquiries relating to this
subject.

In order for Dr Clarke to undertake his research, it will be necessary for him to make requests for access to
MOD material currently closed under the Public Record Act 1958. It is our understanding that from
January 2005 this material will be subject to applications under the Freedom of Information Act, and that
Prior to implementation you will be reviewing information “to see what material may be made more
" ‘generally available.”
Given this commitment, I hope you will consider a) supporting Dr Clarke’s proposal to the E.S.R.C under
the joint scheme and/or b) considering Dr Clarke’s offer of assistance in the process of processing and

cataloguing your archives, as a part of his research proposal.

Yours sincerely,

Dr Joan C. Beal
Director

Subject: Request for release of information

| have just received back the papers which we asked you to look at regarding possible release under the Code of
Practice on Access to Government Information (the Code).

| note that you are happy to release the text of most documents, but wish us to remove any references to people or
departments within the FCO. While we are happy to withhold individuals names (and any unique post identifiers) in
accordance with Exemption 42 of the Code (Privacy of an individual) and the Data Protection Act 1998, we do not
normally remove references to departments from documents we release. Indeed where appropriate we may even
release documents referring to Defence Intelligence branches. Under the Code it is not possible to mearly remove
details without sighting an Exemption of the Code, and being able to justify this action to the Parliamentary
Ombudsman should the requester challenge the decision, something | believe the person requesting this information
is likely to do, particularly if he is unable to tell where the documents have come from.

Please could you let me know as soon as possible, the reason for removing these details and what Exemption of the
Code you believe these should be witheld under.

HWETEATIVE BY GRENADA Oe

1. How to react to further proposals by Grenada, following up their
1977 initiative on UFOs.

Mr Luard expressed the view that
the proposat would bring the UN into disrepute. Accordingly, the UK°

delegation was instructed to oppose’ the draft Grenadan resolution. In
the event, no vote was taken on the resolution; as a compromise, the
General Assembly “studied the text" and requested the Secretary-General |
to transmit it to state members of the UN and to interested specialised

General,

3. The Secretary-General's subsequent report contained onty three replie

from Governments, (India, Luxembourg and the Seychelles) and unforth-
coming reactions from two specialised agencies.

of the one previously circulated.

Subsequent developments are reported
in UKMIS telno 2566 of 27 November.
5. The brief for this item is at flag F.

6. The Grenadan initiative continues to hold no attractions whatsoever
for us. It is obvious, however, that some of. our Western partnérs |

>“ Vincluding:

Gincluding the FRG Presidency and the United States) are making an-
effort to help Grenada off the hook if this can be done without cost
to the UN. It must also be said, in fairness, that the Grenadan
proposal is no.more ridiculous than many other proposals before the
UN. Indeed President Carter has in the past Peportedly taken a
personal interest in the subject of UFOs. The Proposal is at least
not positively harmful to UK interests, whereas taking too strong and
open a stand against Mr Gairy could be. Moreover, if we can help the
Grenadans to reach a compromise, it could conceivably help us to
Persuade them to abstain on the vote on foreign economic and Other
interests in Plenary (the Fourth Committee Resolution which inter alia
strongly condems the UK and others for dealings with South Africa).

7. I recommend that we instruct the Mission to continue to resist the

original Grenadan Proposal but to give discreet Support to efforts by
Western or other countries to postpone or reduce the scope and cost -
of the Grenadan Proposal as much as possible. I accordingly submit a
draft telegram to UKMIS New York.

8. UND were consulted on UN aspects, and concur.

“A UK AIMS

To oppose the establishment of any such agency.

B POINTS TO MAKE

2. The British delegation does not think that the establish—
ment of an agency for research into unidentified flying
objects is appropriate to the functions of the United Nations,
It considers that the exitence of such an agency under UN
auspices would be an unjustifiable drain on UN resources

which could be better deployed elsewhere.

Cc TACTICS

3. Hopefully a confrontation with the representative of

_ Grenada can be avoided, but the UK should not hesitate to

make its views known as and when appropriate. You should
seek whatever support you can particultrly among the Nine.
BACKGROUND

4. At last year's UN General Assembly, the Grenadan
delegation, with the full support of Premier Gairy, sought
to establish an ad hoc working group of the UN to evaluate
UFO reports. FCv Minsiters expressed the view that to set
up any such body would reduce the credibility of the UN;
accordingly the UK delegation was instructed to oppdse.

In the event, no vote was taken on the Grenadan Resolution;
as a@ compromise, the General Assembly "studied the text"

and requested the secretary-General to transmit it "to states
Members of the UN and to interested specialised agencies so

that they might communicate their views to the Secretary-
General". ,

A/SPC/33/L.20)

1. Mr, FRIDAY (Grenada) * read out a letter from L. Gordon Cocper, a former
astronaut, expressing the belief that unidentified flying objects were

extraterrestrial vehicles whose crews were visiting “arth from nore technically

advanced planets. Mr, Cooper had himself spent two days in 1951 observing UFOs of
different sizes flying in fighter formation, generally from east to west over
Europe. A top-level co-ordinated programme was needed to collect and analyse data
from all over the world concerning all types of encounters and to determine how best
to make friendly contact with such visitors. Man might have to prove to the
visitors that he had learned to solve his problems by peaceful means rather than by
warfare before he could be accepted as a fully qualified universal team member. Such
acceptance would have tremendous possibilities for the advancement of the world jin
ell areas, and it would certainly seem that the United Nations had an interest in
handling the subject Properly end expeditiously. If the United Nations agreed to

pursue the project, many more well-qualified people might agree to provide help and
information,

2. Mr. FRIEDMAN (Grenada) * said it vas important to remember that men throuchout
the world had something in common: they were all earthlings, a fact which tended to
be forgotten when man's many differences were aired. After 20 years of study, he
believed that there was overwhelming evidence’ to suggest that the planet was veing
visited by intellicently controlled evtraterrostrinl vehicles. The evidence took the

form of eye-witness testixony fron responsible persons, physical trace cases

producing changes in the environment observable long after the UFO had left, radar
visual sightings, photographs and abductions, apparently by aliens. of earthlings
and the subsequent return of the earthlings, although there was no knowledge of
cases where earthlings had not been returned,

3. Every Large-scale scientific study of UFOs had provided a substantial number of

cases in which the observations clearly indicated that the UFO was a manufactu:
object behaving in

Earth. The abilit
without

red
ways which could not be duplicated by manufactured objects’ on

y of UFOs to move and manoeuvre at thousands of niles an hour,

eny visible external engine, wings or tail, indicated that the spacecraft had
been manufactured by someone other than man, Many people wished to know why UFOs
should visit Earth and what was happening on Earth that could be of interest to an
advanced civilization, Probably there were many things of interest to someone
concerned with the development of a primitive society. However, one thing was
guaranteed to be of interest to another civilization and that was the fact that
within cone hundred years men would be going to the stars. He himself, as a nuclear
physicist, had worked on fission and fusion propulsion systems which would be
capable of transporting man to nearby star systems within reasonable periods of time,
and it would be obvious to any alien in’ the neighbourhood that man would be doing so
within a hundred years. Since the end of the Second World War, with the development

in the light of the importance of questions of peace, survival and hunger, people
should concern themselves with such matters. He suggested that man's very survival
might depend on his taking an earthling orientation, The easiest way of doing so was
for him to try to see himself as others saw him. From up above, coming into the
Planet Earth, there were no boundary lines. Acceptance of oneself as an earthling
would become natural when it was recognized that the planet Earth was being visited,

6. here were also practical aspects such as the significance of man's acquiring
motive propulsion which would enable him to travel and to transport goods from place
to place without using the traditional means’ of transport and without expending :
valuable resources which were normally burned and thrown away, It was clear that -
aliens had solved problems such as the development of new and better means of
producing energy. In addition, the Earth might well have some resources which could. -
be exported to alien civilizations; as the densest planet in the solar system, the
Earth had more valuable heavy metals than any other planet in the neighbourhood and
might also have plant life and other materials which would be of interest to aliens.

working days. We are clearly well over this 20 day deadline already which would
not look particularly favourable if the individual chooses to take his case to the
Parliamentary Ombudsman. | would therefore be grateful if you could give me an
estimate of how long it may be before a decision is made. If there are particular
concerns about these documents or you are having to consult others outside the
FCO, please let me know.

Thank you for your assistance.

Dear Dr Clarke

Thank you for your letter of 3 January, with which you enclosed a cheque for £260 for the papers
sent to you on the 9 December 2002. Please find enclosed a receipt as requested.

With regard to your further points, I will address these in the same order as your letter.

1. We are still awaiting advice on the release of papers from file DS8/75/3 Part A.
I apologise for the delay. As soon as I have a decision, I will write to you. If cleared for release,
we will, of course, send you copies of these papers.

2. We have no record of file $4/4/8/1 amongst our records of surviving ‘UFO’ files.
However, as the document mentioned consultation with DI54 I ask DI staff to search their
archives and the enclosed documents have been located which seem to be relevant to your
enquiry. Personal details have been removed. I have also removed part of paragraph 5, lines 7 to
9 of the loose minute to Head of DIS4 dated 13 December 1977. This is because it contains
information contained in the documents mentioned in paragraph 1 which have not yet been
released. If this information is released to you at a later date, I will also send you another copy of
this document. The original letter from the clergyman was not on the file, but we are making
further enquiries to see if it can be located. I will let you know the outcome of these enquiries as
soon as possible. I have also checked with our archives to see if they hold any files with the
references D/S4(Air)8/2/1C, (mentioned in these papers), $4/4/8/1 or S4(Air)4/8/1, but they been
unable to locate any such surviving files.

3. The papers sent to you from file D/Sec(AS)12/7 wete as requested, copies of reports and
the assessment made. I can confirm that BRRAMeMMeMreport was not seen by ACAS. The Head

of Sec(AS) wrote to ACAS to advise him of these events because around that time there had been
speculation in the media and a parliamentary question by Lord Kennet asking whether USAF
experimental aircraft had been authorised to fly over the UK. ACAS noted this, but had nothing to
add.

4. The file D/Sec(AS)64/2 does contain a copy of the MPs letter to the Department and we
are currently taking advice about its release. 1 will write to you again as soon as possible
regarding this matter.
5. You asked how requests for information will be handled when the Freedom of Information
Act (FOIA) gains full public access in January 2005. Work is currently underway on the
processes to be used when answering Requests for Information (RFI) under the FOIA. Each
request will be dealt with on a case by case basis. Where requests are broad or vague, it is likely
that individuals will be asked to be more specific about the information required in order to
reduce costs, With regard to fees, the Act states that the maximum fee shall not exceed 10% of the
prescribed cost and the disbursements. These include locating and retrieving the information, and

the direct costs of communicating it to the individual concerned. Where the prescribed costs
exceed £600 the Department is not obliged to comply with the request.

6. Finally, you mentioned the future of UFO records that are less than 30 years old. As you
rightly state, many of the files we hold contain the personal details of those who have either
reported their sightings to us, or have corresponded with the MOD and to protect their privacy
these details would have to be removed before the papers could be released. However, in the
remaining time before full implementation of the Act we will be reviewing the information we
hold to see what material may be made more generally available via the MOD Freedom of
Information Publication Scheme. This Scheme was launched on 29 November 2002 as the first
step towards the introduction of the FOIA and can be found at www. foi.mod.uk.

7. I hope this helpful.

Yours sincerely,
From: Directorate of Air Staff (Lower Airspace)

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS

1. You asked me to comment on loose minute DCb3(I)/52 dated 9 December 1977.

As you know, S4&(Air) are responsible for answering public enquiries and for briefing
Ministers on MPs' questions about UFOs, I attach our stock reply which has been
endorsed by successive Ministers. dud Ore. , fr Popes | tung dive de the bnaio Gee
Ap tr EbS a Eeneddynind.

2. So far as I know there has been no British Scientific enquiry into the possible
existence of UFOs, nor is there evidence that MOD has taken the question very
seriously. However, we have kept in touch with the United States Air Force who

made a systematic, if protracted, attempt to record and investigate UFO sightinges
from 1952 to 1968. The USAF enquiry, known as’ Project Blue Book, was followed

by a study which was originally suggested by a member of a congressional investigation
in the Armed Forces Committee and which was assigned by the Air Force Office of
Scientific Research to the University of Colorado. Jt was led by Dr Edward ¥ Condon,
Professor of Physics and formerly Director of the National Bureau of Standards.

This is the. enquiry referred to in the attached brief and, as stated there, it

was endorsed in 1969 by the National Academy of Sciences who concluded that there
was no scientific or military justification for further study of UFOs, that the
reports of UFOs up to that time posed no threat to national security and that
continuance of the Blue Book Project was of doubtful value. The Condon report
caused a good deal of controversy and, I believe, was much criticised. However,
when I consulted the RAF Staff in Washington about this they assured me in

August 1976 that HQ. USAF saw no likelihood of their further involvement in this
area. The USAF pointed to the considerable Air Force conmitment of resources in

the past, the extreme pressure on Air Force funds in 1976 and the fact that since
1969 no evidence had been presented to warrant further investigation.

3. It is always possible of course, that the considerable correspondence addressed
to MOD might contain some hint, not of UFOs, but of something with a hostile
terrestrial origin which could have a bearing on national security. We have
therefore been careful to refer all UFO enquiries from the public to ADISS end DD

Ops (GE)(RAF) for any further investigation which they might consider to be justificd.
As a Secretariat branch, S&(Air) does not need to know about this type of enquiry

and we do not ask for further information; we teil the public flatly that we

cannot enter into detailed correspondence, andjénquiries are not necessarily made

to the point of positive identification. The only exception to this is the

ocensiansl crea where a Harhan af Pantiasant cher mrrrentionel fat

roth gy canst

and we seek ADI 55's advice so that the Minister can reply more fully and’
specifically than the standard draft allows.

4, That is all there really ¢isto the MOD UFO "nolicy" gag¢ept that we do not
publish the correspondence frow the public, ‘he only reason for that is lack
of staff; we would have to oblitevate the identities of our many correspondent:
and possibly occasioual references to classified subjects, and there is no one
we can spare for the job.

5. As it happens, however, I was about to seek confirmation of the general line
we take even before you consulted me on the letter sent to CDS. There is always
a steady public interest in UFOs and from time to time it tends to increase

unaccountably. There was a sustained flurry in the 1960s which was sufficient to
embroil my predecessor in a BBC TV programme. Last summer there was some concern

joker. In the last few wecks the

and he has also had discussions with Mr Jimmy Carter
whom the Press have connected with UFOs in the past. Then a few days ago the

Earl of Glancarty put down a number of questions in the House of Lords referring
to UFO investigations allegedly under way in France and a broadcast said to have

been made in 1974 by the Contemporary French Minister of Defence. (See Hansard
extracts attached).

6. We believe that

is a Vice President of the British UFO Research Association, I should
add that we were unable to verify the alleged Ministerial broadcast when a member
of the public told us in 1976 that in it the French had admitted the existence
of flying saucers. The British Air Attache in Paris did find out however that the
French operate a procedure under which UFO reports from the public go in the first
instance to the French Defence Ministry (Air) who study the military implications
and finally to a Government organisation known as the Centre Nationale D'Etude
Spatial where the reports are considered by scientists. The reports are also
fed into a computer where they are "added to the statistics of the problem",
We were advised that the French military authorities had found nothing of an
aggressive nature in the sightings although their scientists had been unable to
explain the phenomena. As this information came to us by telephone I have now

written to the Air Attache to document it and up date it in the light of the Baril
of Glancarty's questions.

2° One way and another there could be renewed interest in UFOs in the next

few months and I have already been asked to take stock of our current policy.
The basic questions are: -

a. Should we take flying saucers seriously?

b. Is the general MOD policy valid (as outlined in the attached brief and
a@s supplemented above)?

c. Is it correct to confine MOD investigations to the defence implications
of alleged UFO sightings ~- should the investigations be extended either in
the interest of national defence or more widely into the more speculative
field concerning the possible existence of extra terrestrial vehicles?

d. Should MOD take the public more seriously and, for example, visit the
places where sightings have been reported (a line of enquiry; I might add
which could not be undertaken by S4(Air)'s slender establishment)?
8. I should be most grateful if you would seek the views of DSTI and Air
Cdre (Int) whom I believe you will be consulting before reporting to CDS.

2. Simple explanations have been found for the great majority of UFO reports,

the most common Single source being aireraft or the lights of eircraft seen under

urmisuel conditions, Other common explanations are astronomical Sightings, space
satellites or space junk, belloons, unusual cloud formations or aircraft
condensation trails. Unexplained reports have often been found to be imprecise

or lacking in some essential data, but othe wise to have been no different

from other reports, :

3. I am of course aware of work that has been done elsewhere, in particular the
studies of the University of Colorado, whose main findings were endorsed by a panel

of the US National Academy of Sciences. The Conclusions the panel came to were:

ordinary phenomena;

that added to scientific knowledge ;

are persuesive and I would not Gisagree with any of their findings.

Lord HARRIS of GREENWICH:

The Government has no knowledge of
either the radio interview to which the
Question refers, or the role played by the
gendarmerie in investigating unidentified
flying objects. The police in this country
have not been asked to collect reports of,
or investigate, unidentified flying objects.
The jurisdiction and powers of the police
are normally confined to terrestrial activi-
ties, but | have every confidence that
should an occasion arise where there is
evidence that an unidentified flying object
has landed within a police area, the police
force concerned will investigate it with its
customary vigour, However, until there
is some clear indication that the frequency
of such occurrences is likely to impose a
significant burden on the police, 1 doubt
whether it would prove fruitful to issue
guidance on this subject.

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS

4. You asked me to comment on loose minute DCDS(I)/52 dated 9 December 1977,

As you know, S4(Air) are responsible for answering public enquiries and for briefing

Ministers on MPs' questions about UFOs. I attach our stock reply which has been

endorsed by successive Ministers,- dud elves , ¥ Fr Gps | tage Live he the Carded Ge
Ly GBS 4 Eenedpynctint,

2. So far as I know there has been no British Scientific enquiry into the possible

existence of UFOs, nor is there evidence that MOD has taken the question very

seriously. However, we have kept in touch with the United States Air Force who

made a systematic, if protracted, attempt to record and investigate UFO sightines

from 1952 to 1968. The USAF enquiry, known as Project Blue Book, was followed

by a study which was originally suggested by a member of a congressional investigation

in the Armed Forces Committee and which was assigned by the Air Force Office of

Selentific Research to the University of Colorado. It was led by Dr Edward V Condon,

Professor of Physics and formerly Director of the National Bureau of Standards.

This is the enquiry referred to in the attached brief and, as stated there, it

was endorsed in 1969 by the National Academy of Sciences who concluded that there

was no scientific or military justification for further study of UFOs, that the

reports of UFOs up to that time posed no threat to national security and that

continuance of the Blue Book Project was of doubtful value. The Condon report

caused a good deal of controversy and, I believe, was much criticised. However,

when I consulted the RAF Staff in Washington about this they assured me in

August 1976 that HQ USAF saw no likelihood of their further involvement in this

area. The USAF pointed to the considerable Air Force commitment of resources in

the past, the extreme pressure on Air Force funds in 1976 and the fact that since

1969 no evidence had been presented to warrant further investigation.

3. It is always possible of course, that the considerable correspondence addressed
to MOD might contain some hint, not of UFOs, but of something with a hostile
terrestrial origin which could have a bearing on national security. We have

therefore been careful to refer all UFO enquiries from the public to ADI55 and DD

Ops (GE)(RAF) for any further investigation which they might consider to be justified.
As a Secretariat branch, S4(Air) does not need to know about this type of enquiry

and we do not ask for further information; we foil the public flatly that we

cannot enter into detailed correspondence, andjehouiries are not necessarily made

to the point of positive identification. The onty exception to this is the
ocersional case where a Merker af Darliomant chee at :
and we seek ADI 55's advice so that the Minister can veply t more fully and
specifically than the standsrd draft allows.

4, hat is all there really #isto the MOD UFO "nolicy" gept that we do not
publish the correspondence from the public. ‘he only reason for that is lack
of staff; we would have to obliterate the identities of our many correspondent,
and possibly occasional references to classified subjects, and there is no one
we can spare for the job.
5. As it happens, however, I was about to seek confirmation of the general line
we take even before you consulted me on the letter sent to CDS, There is always
a steady public interest in UFOs and from time to time it tends to increase
unaccountably. There was a sustained flurry in the 1960s which was sufficient to
embroil my predecessor in a BBC TV programme. Last summer there was some concern

in Wales although the RAF Police thought this could have been the work of a practical

joker. In the last few weeks the Prime Minister of Grenada has secured a UFO
debate at the United Nations (which could concewably involve. the Foreign Office
and No 10 in correspondence) and he has also had discussions with Mr Jimmy Carter
whom the Press have connected with UFOs in the past. Then a few days ago the
Earl of Glancarty put down a number of questions in the House of Lords referring
to UFO investigations allegedly under way in France and a broadcast said to have
been made in 1974 by the Contemporary French Minister of Defence. (See Hansard
extracts attached).

6. We believe that the Earl's half brother and heir, the Hon Brinsley Le Poer

French, is a Vice President of the British UFO Research Association. I should
add that we were unable to verify the alleged Ministerial broadcast when a member
of the public told us in 1976 that in it the French had admitted the existence

of flying saucers. The British Air Attache in Paris did find out however that the
French operate a procedure under which UFO reports from the public go in the first
instance to the French Defence Ministry (Air) who study the military implications
and finally to a Government organisation known as the Centre Nationale D'Etude
Spatial where the reports are considered by scientists, The reports are also

fed into a computer where they are "added to the statistics of the problem",

We were advised that the French military authorities had found nothing of an
aggressive nature in the sightings although their scientists had been unable to
explain the phenomena. As this information came to us by telephone I have now
written to the Air Attache to document it and up date it in the light of the Earl
of Glancarty's questions.

Te One way and another there could be renewed interest in UFOs in the next
few months and I have already been asked to take stock of our current policy.
The basic questions are: -

a. Should we take flying saucers seriously?

bs. Is the general MOD policy valid (as outlined in the attached brief and
as supplemented above)? ;

Co Is it correct to confine MOD investigations to the defence implications
of alleged UFO sightings - should the investigations be extended either in
_the interest of national defence or more widely into the more speculative
field concerning the possible existence of extra terrestrial vehicles?

d. Should MOD take the public more seriously and, for example, visit the
places where sightings have been reported (a line of enquiry; I might add
which could not be undertaken by S4(Air)'s slender establishment)?
8. I should be most grateful if you would seek the views of DSTI and Air
Cdre (Int) whom I believe you will be consulting before reporting to CDs.

possible existence of UFOs, nor is there evidence that HCD has taken the question
very seriously. However, the MOR has kept in tcuch with the United States Air Farce

cone juded that there was no scientific or military justification for further study
of UFOs, that the reports of UFOs up to that time posed no threat to national
security and that continuance of the Blue Book Project was of doubtful value.

The report caused a good deal of controversy and, I believe, was much criticised.
However, when the RAP steff in Washington was consulted in April 4976 about this
they assured MOD that HQ USAF saw no likelihood of their further involvement in this
ar@ae ‘The USAF pointed to the considerable Air Force commitment of resources in

DRAET REPLY TO LETTER FROM THE REV ERIC INGLESBY

4. An MOD study of the scientific significance of UFC's has not been

earried out since our interest is limited to the defence aspects. We receive

UFO reports from members of the public, the police and Service units and our

sole aim is to cstablish any possible defence implications. We have not the
resources to undertake the investigation to the point of positive correlation

with a known object anc there is no organisation appointed for the study

of UFGs, per se, UFO reports are exsemined by various specialist staff within the
Ministry of Defence as part of their normal staff responsibilities, They can call
on the full seientific and professional. resources of the Department and if necessary

may call on the expert advice of other Government ard non-Government bodies,

2. Simple explanations have been fourd for the great majority of UFO reports,

the most common single source being airoraft or the lights of eircraft seen under
unusuel conditions. Other common explanations are astronomical sightings, space
satellites or space junk, balloons, unusual cloud formations or aircraft
condensation trails. Unexplained reports have often been found to be imprecise
or lacking in some ¢ssential data, but otherwise to have been no different ain kind
from other reports,

3. I am of course aware of work that has been done elsewhere, in particular the
studies of the University of Colorado, whose main findings were endorsed by a panel

of the US National Academy of Sciences. The conclusions the panel came to were:

a) about 90% of all UFO reports proved to be quite plausibly related to

ordinary phenomena};

b) little, if anything, had come from the study of UFOs during 21 years

thet added to scientific knowledge;
c) further extensive ctudy of UFO sightings was not justified in the

expectation that scicnee would be advanced thereby;

a) no evidence had come to light that UFO sigh tings might represent

a defence hazard.

4, Although I would not dismiss the possibility that intelligent life may exist
in outer space, the UFO reports that have reached the MOD to cate contain no

evidence to support this hypothesis, The conclusions of the American work

are persuasive and I would not disagree with any of their findings.

Lord HARRIS. of GREENWICH:
The Government has po knowledge of
either the radio interview to which the
Question refers, or the role played by the
gendarmerie in investigating unidentified
flying objects. The police in this country
have not been asked to collect reports of,

are normally confined to terrestrial activi-
ties, but I have every confidence that
should an occasion arise where there is
evidence that an unidentified flying object
has landed within a police area, the police
force concerned will investigate it with its
customary vigour. However, until there
is some clear indication that the frequency
of such occurrences is fikely to impose a
significant burden on the police, 1 doubt
whether it would prove fruitful to issue
guidance on this subject.

THE REAL UFOs

139

would it be seen flying low over the coast near Haverfordwest,
because the debris didn’t come down anywhere near Britain;
neither can a piece of even the smallest space debris hover — it
falls with the speed dictated by gravity.

I took an unprecedented step and ordered a number of radar
tapes to be impounded and sent to me. As these tapes are usually
wiped for reuse, it was important to work fast. There were a
few returns which fitted the times and locations when sightings
were made and after several hours of scouring the standard
VHS videos I could isolate and identify these. At first, the
results were disappointing. The blips faded in and out all
night, like ghosts in the morning light. RAF radar experts
explained these conventionally enough. Ground clutter, they
said, tall trees picked up now and again around one particular
radar head. But the frustration turned to fear: there were too
many visual sightings, and the reports were from witnesses too
trustworthy to ignore. Whatever it was that zigzagged Britain
on 30 and 31 March 1993, that probed our fields and raced our
cars, it was not picked up by radar. And consequently, with no
radar track to set the procedure in motion, we hadn’t even got
our aircraft into the air. Was this the same triangle that had been
seen over Belgium three years earlier? And could it now evade
radar altogether?

Over the coming weeks I tried to find an explanation, but every
avenue led nowhere. Whatever it was had come and gone. It
was time to take the whole problem ‘upstairs’. Frankly, I didn’t
hold out much hope that my bosses would listen. As I have
said, my hands-on approach and my firm views that we were
facing in UFOs a genuine phenomenon that needed serious and
urgent research had not met with popularity in Secretariat (Air
Staff). Subtlety was the key word, I felt. I drew up a carefully
constructed report of the 30-31 March sightings and sent it to my
head of division. I deliberately avoided the emotive word ‘UFO’,

OPEN SKIES, CLOSED MINDS

140

inspirer of prejudices, and opted instead for ‘uncorrelated target’
and ‘unknown craft’. Government bureaucracies are sometimes
accused of inventing jargon for the sake of it, but here I felt it
was necessary, it paid off. The report was passed up the chain
of command until it reached the assistant chief of the air staff
himself.

Simultaneously, I contacted the American embassy and asked
them whether an unusual prototype aircraft of American con-
struction was operating over Britain and might explain the
various sightings. There had been rumours for months in
the corridors of power that an aircraft called Aurora, which
would make the Stealth bomber look like a Sopwith Pup, was
in production. There had been consistent denials everywhere,
however. A high-tech, radar-evading craft capable of great speeds
and manoeuvrability, the sort of machine Clint Eastwood flies in
Firefox, belonged to fiction. Aurora, we were told, did not exist.
The Americans were as nonplussed as we and the Belgians were
by the sightings.

The assistant chief of the air staff noted my report — there was
little else he could do. By now I had tried all possible lines of
inquiry. There were no other avenues left.

So the official findings (mine) read: ‘Type of craft — unknown;
origin of craft - unknown; motive of occupants — unknown.’
And, although it appears nowhere in the official documentation,
I would have to add: ‘Conclusion — unsatisfactory.’

The 30-31 March sightings brought about a marked change in
my own attitude. I would play no further part in bland platitudes
about UFOs being ‘of no defence significance’. I sensed that some
of my colleagues thought UFOs were only of defence significance
if they aimed laser beams at cities. But any craft, conventional or
otherwise, that can do what that triangle did is of extreme defence
significance in itself. Our radar couldn’t trace it, our jets wouldn’t
be able to catch it. We can all thank our God — or our lucky stars

— that whatever it was it \

Your ref: D/DAS/64/3/11

3 January, 2003

Dear

Thank you for your letter of 9 December enclosing the copies of UFO related documents
Thad requested under the Code of Practice.

Having had the opportunity to examine the material in depth during the holidays, I wish
to thank you and your colleagues for the thorough and highly efficient manner in which
you have handled this application.

As requested I enclose a crossed cheque for the £260 cost of processing, made out to the
Accounting Officer MoD. I would be grateful if you could supply me with a receipt,
which is required for my research project accounts. I have a number of matters arising
from an examination of the material which I will address point by point:

1. Inote with reference to file D/DS8/75/3 Part A (Parliamentary Correspondence
file, House of Lords debate, 1977-79), that several background documents generated
by another Government department relating to the debate are being examined for
possible release. If they are cleared for release, I would certainly wish to receive
copies of these outstanding papers.

2. With reference to the enclosure ‘To the Head of S4’ dated 2 March 1978 included in
the Parliamentary Correspondence file (highlighted copy enclosed): I draw your
attention to the line which reads “...head of DI 54 consulted us at around the same
time because he had been asked to draft a reply for CDS to send to a clergyman
enquiring about UFOs. I took the opportunity to confirm our procedures on this
subject ~ he has confirmed that we need do no more than look for defence
implications. The key papers are Encl 71 and 78 on file S4/4/8/1 — Flags Y and Z.”
As far as I can recall this is the first time during my research that I have seen a
reference to the Chief of Defence Staff being consulted with regards to policy on

UFOs. This leads me to ask: does file ‘S4/4/8/1’ exist in your archive, and if so can
copies of the relevant file, particularly ‘Enclosures 71 and 78’, be supplied?

3. With reference to File D/Sec(AS)12/7, UFO Report 31 March 1993. Thank you for
enclosing the loose minute showing inquiries made and details of the radar replay
relevant to the investigation. You may be aware that the incumbent at Sec(AS)2a
during 1993, Nick Pope, refers to his investigations into these reports in his book
published in 1996, Open Skies Closed Minds. In the book (pg 140) he refers to the
report he compiled that was sent to the head of his division, a copy of which you
enclosed (dated 16 April 1993). Mr Pope states this report was “passed up the chain
of command until it reached the assistant chief of the air staff himself’ (presumably
Air Marshal Sir Timothy Garden). However, there is no evidence in the file you
released to suggested ACAS saw or commented on Mr Pope’s report. Could you
confirm that is the case?

4. With reference to File D/Sec(AS)64/2 Part D, re UFO sightings, early hours of 5
October 1996. I’m grateful that you enclosed the loose minute which responds to
press reports criticising UK air defence systems arising from these events. My
background research suggests the source of these criticisms was the late Martin
Redmond MP, who asked a series of PQs on related matters in 1996. The LM dated
13 November 1996 you enclosed responds to “Mr Redmond’s letter” but it is difficult
to follow the responses without reference to the letter itself. Is a copy of Redmond’s
original letter enclosed in the file, and if so could a copy be supplied?

Finally, thank you for your detailed response to my questions on the impact of the FOIA
upon the ’30 year rule.’ However, I remain unsure as to how the new act would work in
practical terms. If in January 2005 I was to apply, for example, for access to the MoD’s
UFO records for the year 1981, how would my request be processed? Would the present
procedure whereby pages are checked, sanitised and a charge made, continue? If that is
the case, such requests might have to be rejected because of cost or staff time, which
seems to defeat the objectives of the FOIA.

It seems to me that the single remaining problem with the release of UFO records
younger than 30 years relate to the issue of personal information, ie informants names
and addresses, rather than because of any security issues. Does the MoD have any
suggestions as to how this problem could be overcome after 2005?

In the meantime, thank you for your attention to my requests and a happy New Year,

Yours sincerely,
